Model Checking Temporal Properties of Recursive Probabilistic Programs Thanks: This work is supported by the DFG Research Training Group 2236 UnRAVeL and the ERC Advanced Grant 787914 FRAPPANT
Tobias Winkler ORCID:0000-0003-1084-6408, Christina Gehnen ORCID:0000-0002-6548-3432, Joost-Pieter Katoen ORCID:0000-0002-6143-1926

Abstract
. Probabilistic pushdown automata (pPDA) are a standard operational model for programming languages involving discrete random choices and recursive procedures. Temporal properties are useful for specifying the chronological order of events during program execution. Existing approaches for model checking pPDA against temporal properties have focused mostly on ω \omega -regular and LTL properties. In this paper, we give decidability and complexity results for the model checking problem of pPDA against ω \omega -visibly pushdown languages that can be described by specification logics such as CaRet. These logical formulae allow specifying properties that explicitly take the structured computations arising from procedural programs into account. For example, CaRet is able to match procedure calls with their corresponding future returns, and thus allows to express fundamental program properties such as total and partial correctness.

1.
Introduction

Probabilistic programs
extend traditional programs with the ability to flip coins or, more generally, sample values from probability distributions.
These programs can be used to encode randomized algorithms and mechanisms in security
[
DBLP:journals/toplas/BartheKOB13
]
in a natural way.
The interest in probabilistic programs has significantly increased in recent years.
To a large extent, this is due to the search in AI for more expressive and succinct languages than probabilistic graphical models for Bayesian inference
[
DBLP:conf/icse/GordonHNR14
]
.
Probabilistic programs have many applications
[
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-1809-10756
]
.
They are used in, among other areas, machine learning, systems biology, security, planning and control, quantum computing, and software–defined networks.
Probabilistic variants of many programming languages exist.

Recursion

Procedural programs
allow for the declaration of procedures—small independent code blocks—and the ability to
call
procedures from one another, possibly in a recursive fashion.
Most common programming languages such as C, Python, or Java support procedures.
It is thus not surprising that recursion is also present in many modern probabilistic programming languages (PPL) such as WebPPL
[
dippl
]
or Church
[
DBLP:conf/starai/StuhlmullerG12
]
.
In fact, there have been numerous approaches to extend Bayesian networks with recursion even before PPL became popular
[
DBLP:conf/aaai/PfefferK00
,
DBLP:journals/amai/Jaeger01
,
Cassini2008
]
.
Randomized algorithms such as Hoare’s quicksort (see, e.g.,
[
DBLP:journals/dam/Karp91
]
) with random pivot selection can be readily implemented using recursion.
Finally, recursion in form of branching processes is an important tool to model reproduction of cells or molecules in systems biology
[
axelrod2015branching
]
.

\displaystyle\texttt{proc}~\texttt{void}~\mathit{infectYoung}():

\displaystyle\qquad y\coloneqq\texttt{uniform}(0,3)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{repeat}~y~\texttt{times}:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathit{infectYoung}()

\displaystyle\qquad e\coloneqq\texttt{uniform}(0,2)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{repeat}~e~\texttt{times}:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad f\coloneqq\mathit{infectElder}()

return

\displaystyle\texttt{proc}~\texttt{bool}~\mathit{infectElder}():

\displaystyle\qquad y\coloneqq\texttt{uniform}(0,1)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{repeat}~y~\texttt{times}:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathit{infectYoung}()

\displaystyle\qquad e\coloneqq\texttt{uniform}(0,4)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{repeat}~e~\texttt{times}:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathit{infectElder}()

\displaystyle\qquad f\coloneqq\texttt{bernoulli}(0.01)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{return}~f

Figure 1.

Recursive probabilistic program modeling the outbreak of an infectious disease.

\texttt{uniform}(a,b)
stands for the
discrete
uniform distribution on
[a,b]
.

Motivating example

This paper studies the automated verification of
probabilistic pushdown automata
(pPDA)
[
esparzaMCPPDA-lics
]
as an explicit-state operational model of procedural probabilistic programs against temporal specifications.
As a motivating example we consider a simple epidemiological model for the outbreak of an infectious disease in a large population where the number of susceptible individuals can be assumed to be infinite.
Our example model distinguishes young and elderly persons.
Each affected individual infects a uniformly distributed number of others, with varying rates (expected values) according to the age groups (
Figure

2
).
The fatality rate for infected elderly and young persons is 1% and 0%, respectively.
Procedure
\mathit{infectElder()}
returns a Boolean in order to signal to its callers whether the infection has led to fatality.
Initially, we assume there is a single infected young person, i.e., the overall program is started by calling
\mathit{infectYoung()}
.
It is an easy exercise to specify this model as a discrete probabilistic program with mutually recursive procedures (
Figure

1
).
Note that this program can be easily amended to more realistic scenarios involving, e.g., more age or gender groups, hospitalization rate, etc.

The behavior of such recursive probabilistic programs can be naturally described by pPDA.
Roughly, the
local
states of the procedures—the values of the variables in the procedure’s scope and the position of the program counter—constitute both the state space and the stack alphabet of the automaton.
Procedure calls correspond to push transitions in the pPDA in such a way that the program’s
procedure stack
is simulated by the automaton’s pushdown stack, i.e., the caller’s current local state is saved on top of the stack.
Accordingly,
returning
from a procedure corresponds to taking a pop transition in order to restore the local state of the caller.
Returning a value can be handled similarly.
Clearly, if the reachable local state spaces of the involved procedures are finite, then the resulting automaton will be finite as well.
We refer to
[
DBLP:reference/mc/AlurBE18
]
for more details.

A number of natural questions such as “Will the virus eventually become extinct?” (termination probability) or “What is the expected number of fatalities?” (expected costs) are decidable for finite pPDA (see
[
brazdilSurvey
]
for a survey).
In this work, we focus on
temporal properties
, i.e., questions that involve reasoning about the chronological order of events during the epidemic.
An example are chains of infection:
For instance, we might ask

What’s the probability that eventually a young person with only young persons in their chain of infection passes the virus on to an elderly person who then dies?

On the level of the program in
Figure

1
, this corresponds to the probability of reaching a
global
program configuration where the call stack only contains
\mathit{infectYoung}()
invocations and during execution of the current
\mathit{infectYoung}()
, the local variable
f
is eventually set to true.
This requires reasoning about the nestings of calls and returns of a computation.
In fact, in order to decide if
f=\mathit{true}
in the current procedure, we must “skip” over all calls within it and only consider their local return values.
This requirement (and many others) can be naturally expressed in the logic
CaRet

[
caret
]
, an extension of LTL:

\lozenge^{g}\,(\,\square^{-}p_{Y}~\land~p_{Y}~\land~\lozenge^{a}f\,)~.

Here,
p_{Y}
is an atomic proposition that holds at states which correspond to being in procedure
\mathit{infectYoung}
, and
f
indicates that
f=\mathit{true}
.
Intuitively, the above formula states that eventually (outer
\lozenge^{g}
), the computation reaches a (global) state where only
\mathit{infectYoung}
is on the call stack and the current procedure is
\mathit{infectYoung}
as well (
\square^{-}p_{Y}~\land~p_{Y}
), and moreover the local—aka
abstract
—path
within
in the current procedure reaches a state where
f
is true (
\lozenge^{a}f
).
Such properties are in general context-free but not always regular and thus cannot be expressed in LTL
[
caret
]
.

Contributions

The contribution of this paper is a solution to the following problem:

Given a (finite) pPDA
\Delta
and an
\omega
-visibly pushdown language (VPL)
\mathcal{L}
in terms of either a CaRet formula or an automaton, determine the probability that a random trajectory of
\Delta
is in
\mathcal{L}
.

The complexity results for the associated decision problems are summarized in
Table

1
.
As common in the literature, we consider the special case of qualitative, i.e.,
almost-sure
model checking separately.
To the best of our knowledge, none of the problems in
Table

1
was known to be decidable before.
The work of
[
dubslaff
]
proved decidability of model checking against deterministic Muller
visibly pushdown automata
(VPA) which capture a strict subset of the CaRet-definable languages
[
vpl
]
.
The most important technical insight of this paper is that two existing (but independently developed) constructions from the literature can be combined to enable effective model checking of pPDA against
\omega
-VPL:
The
deterministic stair-parity VPA
introduced in
[
vpGames
]
, and a certain
finite Markov chain
associated with a pPDA
[
esparzaMCPPDA-lics
]
.
We provide some more details in the next paragraph.

Table 1.

Complexity results established in this paper.

\omega
-VPL given in terms of …

qualitative

quantitative

Deterministic stair-parity VPA [
]

in
\mathsf{PSPACE}

in
\mathsf{PSPACE}

Non-deterministic Büchi VPA [
]

\mathsf{EXPTIME}
-compl.

in
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}

CaRet formula [
]

in
\mathsf{2EXPTIME}

in
\mathsf{2EXPSPACE}

Techniques and tools

We briefly outline our approach which is built on a number of existing constructions and results from the literature.
In order for the model checking problems to be decidable
[
dubslaff
]
, we need to impose a mild
visibility
restriction on
\Delta
, yielding a probabilistic
visibly
pushdown automaton (pVPA).
Just like several previous works on model checking pPDA against
\omega
-regular specifications
[
esparzaMCPPDA-lics
,
BKS05
,
esparzaMCPPDA
]
, we follow an automata-based approach (see
Figure

3
).
More specifically, we first translate
\varphi
into an equivalent non-deterministic
Büchi
VPA
[
vpl
]

\mathcal{A}
and then determinize it using a procedure introduced by Löding et al.
[
vpGames
]
.
The resulting DVPA
\mathcal{D}
uses a so-called
stair-parity

[
vpGames
]
acceptance condition that is strictly more expressive than standard parity or Muller DVPA
[
vpl
]
.
Stair-parity differs from usual parity in that it only considers certain positions—called
steps

[
vpGames
]
—of an infinite word where the stack height never decreases again.
We then construct a standard product
\Delta\times\mathcal{D}
.
Here, the visibility conditions ensure that the automata synchronize their stack actions, yielding a product automaton that uses
a single stack
instead of two independent stacks, which would lead to undecidability
[
dubslaff
]
.
Finally, we are left with computing a stair-parity acceptance probability in the product.
This is achieved by constructing a specific finite Markov chain associated to
\Delta\times\mathcal{D}
, called
step chain
in this paper.
Intuitively, the step chain “jumps” from one step of a run to the next, hence we only need to evaluate
standard parity
on the step chain.
The idea of step chains was introduced by Esparza et al.
[
esparzaMCPPDA-lics
]
who used them to show decidability of the model checking problem against deterministic (non-pushdown) Büchi automata.
For constructing the step chain, certain
reachability probabilities
in the given pPDA need to be computed.
These probabilities are algebraic numbers (i.e., solutions of polynomial equations) that may be irrational in general.
However, the relevant problems are still decidable via an encoding in the existential fragment of the FO-theory of the reals (ETR)
[
esparzaMCPPDA
]
.

CaRet
formula
\varphi
(Def.
2.8
)
Büchi
NVPA
\mathcal{A}
(Def.
2.1
)
Stair-parity
DVPA
\mathcal{D}
(Def.
2.6
)
Product
\Delta\times\mathcal{D}
(Def.
)
pVPA
\Delta
(Def.
3.1
)
Program
Step chain
\mathcal{M}_{\Delta\times\mathcal{D}}
(Def.
)
ETR

Figure 3.
Chain of reductions used in this paper.
ETR stands for
existential theory of the reals
, i.e., the existentially quantified fragment of the FO-theory over
(\mathbb{R},+,\cdot,\leq)
.

Related work.

We have already mentioned various works on recursion in probabilistic graphical models and PPL as well as on verifying pPDA and the equivalent model of recursive Markov chains
[
DBLP:journals/jacm/EtessamiY09
]
.
The analysis of these models focuses on reachability probabilities,
\omega
-regular properties or (fragments of) probabilistic CTL, expected costs, and termination probabilities.
The computation of termination probabilities in recursive Markov chains and variations thereof with non-determinism is supported by the software tool PReMo
[
DBLP:conf/tacas/WojtczakE07
]
.
Our paper can be seen as a natural extension from checking pPDA against
\omega
-regular properties to
\omega
-visibly pushdown languages.
In contrast to these algorithmic approaches, various deductive reasoning methods have been developed for recursive probabilistic programs.
Proof rules for recursion were first provided in
[
DBLP:phd/ethos/Jones90
]
, and later extended to proof rules in a weakest-precondition reasoning style
[
DBLP:journals/tcs/McIverM01a
,
DBLP:conf/lics/OlmedoKKM16
]
.
The authors of
[
DBLP:conf/lics/OlmedoKKM16
]
also address the connection to pPDA and provide proof rules for expected run-time analysis.
A mechanized method for proving properties of randomized algorithms, including recursive ones, for the Coq proof assistant is presented in
[
DBLP:journals/scp/AudebaudP09
]
.
The Coq approach is based on higher-order logic using a monadic interpretation of programs as probabilistic distributions.

Conference version

A preliminary version of this article was published at FoSSaCS 2022
[
fossacs
]
.
The present journal version extends the conference paper by the full proofs as well as further examples and explanations.

Paper structure

This paper is structured as follows.
We review the basics about VPA and CaRet in
Section

2
.

Section

3
introduces probabilistic
visibly
pushdown automata (pVPA).
The stair-parity DVPA model checking procedure is presented in
, and the results for Büchi VPA and CaRet in
.
We conclude the paper in
.

2.
Visibly Pushdown Languages

In this section, we summarize some preliminary results on visibly pushdown languages and their corresponding automata models, and we recall the syntax and semantics of CaRet.

We use the following notation for words.
Given a non-empty alphabet
\Sigma
, let
\Sigma^{*}
be the set of all finite words (including the empty word
\epsilon
), and let
\Sigma^{\omega}
be the set of all infinite words over
\Sigma
.
For
i\geq 0
, the
i
-th symbol of a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
is denoted
w(i)
if it exists.

|w|
denotes the length of
w
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we write
\Sigma^{n}=\{w\in\Sigma^{*}\mid|w|=n\}
.
For sets of words
A\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
and
B\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
, the concatenation of all words from
A
with those from
B
is denoted
A.B
.
We also use
a.B
and
A.b
as shorthands for
\{a\}.B
and
A.\{b\}
, respectively.

2.1.
Visibly Pushdown Automata

A finite alphabet
\Sigma
is called
pushdown alphabet
if it is equipped with a partition
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}\uplus\Sigma_{\mathsf{int}}\uplus\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}
into three—possibly empty—subsets of
call
,
internal
, and
return
symbols.
A
visibly pushdown automaton
(VPA) over
\Sigma
is like a standard pushdown automaton with the additional syntactic restriction that reading a call, internal, or return symbol triggers a push, internal, or pop transition, respectively (an internal transition is one that does not change the stack height).
Formally:

Definition 2.1

(VPA
[
vpl
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a pushdown alphabet.
A
visibly pushdown automaton (VPA)
over
\Sigma
is a tuple

\mathcal{A}=(S,\,s_{0},\,\Gamma,\,\bot,\,\delta,\,\Sigma)

with
S
a finite set of states,

s_{0}\in S
an initial state,

\Gamma
a finite stack alphabet,

\bot\in\Gamma
a special bottom-of-stack symbol, and

\delta=(\delta_{\mathsf{call}},\delta_{\mathsf{int}},\delta_{\mathsf{ret}})
a triple of relations such that

\delta_{\mathsf{call}}\subseteq(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}})\times(S\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot})~,\quad\delta_{\mathsf{int}}\subseteq(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{int}})\times S~,\quad\delta_{\mathsf{ret}}\subseteq(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}\times\Gamma)\times S

where
\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}=\Gamma\setminus\{\bot\}
.

\triangle

For
s,t\in S
,
Z\in\Gamma
, and
a\in\Sigma
, we write

s\xrightarrow{a}tZ
,
s\xrightarrow{a}t
,
sZ\xrightarrow{a}t
to indicate that there exist transitions

(s,a,t,Z)\in\delta_{\mathsf{call}}
,
(s,a,t)\in\delta_{\mathsf{int}}
,
(s,a,Z,t)\in\delta_{\mathsf{ret}}
, respectively.

The semantics of a VPA is defined as usual via configurations and runs.
A
configuration
of VPA
\mathcal{A}
is a tuple
(s,\gamma)\in S\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{*}.\bot
, written more succinctly as
s\gamma
in the sequel.
Intuitively, being in configuration
s\gamma
means that the automaton is currently in state
s
and has the word
\gamma
on its stack.
If
s\gamma=sZ\gamma^{\prime}
for some
Z\in\Gamma
, then
sZ
is called the
head
of
s\gamma
.
A
bottom configuration
is a configuration with head
s\bot
for some
s\in S
.
Let
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
be an infinite input word.
An infinite sequence
\rho=s_{0}\gamma_{0},s_{1}\gamma_{1}\ldots
of configurations is called a
run
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
if
s_{0}\gamma_{0}=s_{0}\bot
and for all
i\geq 0
, exactly one of the following cases applies:

•

w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}
and
\gamma_{i+1}=Z\gamma_{i}
for some
Z\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}
such that
s_{i}\xrightarrow{w(i)}s_{i+1}Z
; or

•

w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{int}}
and
\gamma_{i+1}=\gamma_{i}
and
s_{i}\xrightarrow{w(i)}s_{i+1}
; or

•

w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}
and
Z\gamma_{i+1}=\gamma_{i}
for some
Z\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}
such that
s_{i}Z\xrightarrow{w(i)}s_{i+1}
; or

•

w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}
and
\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{i+1}=\bot
and
s_{i}\bot\xrightarrow{w(i)}s_{i+1}
.

In other words, if
\mathcal{A}
reads a call (or internal) symbol
a
while being in configuration
s\gamma
and there exists a suitable call transition
s\xrightarrow{a}tZ
(or internal transition
s\xrightarrow{a}t
), then a run of
\mathcal{A}
may evolve from configuration
s\gamma
to
tZ\gamma
(or
t\gamma
, respectively).
Similarly, if
\mathcal{A}
reads a return symbol
a
in configuration
sZ\gamma
where
Z\neq\bot
and there is a transition
sZ\xrightarrow{a}t
, then a run can move from
sZ\gamma
to
t\gamma
.
Note that invoking a return transition in a bottom configuration
s\bot
does not remove the topmost symbol
\bot
from the stack.

A
Büchi acceptance condition
for
\mathcal{A}
is a subset
F\subseteq S
.
A VPA equipped with a Büchi condition is called a
Büchi VPA
.
An infinite word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
is accepted by a Büchi VPA if there exists a run
s_{0}\gamma_{0},s_{1}\gamma_{1},\ldots
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
such that
s_{i}\in F
for infinitely many
i\geq 0
.
The
\omega
-language of words accepted by a Büchi VPA
\mathcal{A}
is denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
.

Definition 2.2

(
\omega
-VPL
[
vpl
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a pushdown alphabet.

L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is an
\omega
-visibly pushdown language (
\omega
-VPL)
if
L=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
for a Büchi VPA
\mathcal{A}
over
\Sigma
.

\triangle

A VPA is
deterministic
(DVPA) if the relations
\delta_{\mathsf{call}},\delta_{\mathsf{int}}
, and
\delta_{\mathsf{ret}}
are total functions, i.e.,

\delta_{\mathsf{call}}\colon(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}})\to(S\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}),\delta_{\mathsf{int}}\colon(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{int}})\to S
,
and

\delta_{\mathsf{ret}}\colon(S\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}\times\Gamma)\to S
.
Note that DVPA have exactly one run on each input word.
As for standard NBA, the class of languages recognized by Büchi DVPA is a strict subset of the languages recognized by non-deterministic Büchi VPA.
Unlike in the non-pushdown case, DVPA with Muller or parity conditions are also strictly less expressive than non-deterministic Büchi VPA
[
vpl
]
.
A deterministic automaton model for
\omega
-VPL was given in
[
vpGames
]
.
It uses a so-called
stair-parity
acceptance condition which we explain in the upcoming
Section

2.2
.

2.2.
Steps and Stair-parity Conditions

In the remainder of this section,
\Sigma
denotes a pushdown alphabet and
\mathcal{A}
a VPA over
\Sigma
.
Consider a run
\rho=s_{0}\gamma_{0},s_{1}\gamma_{1},\ldots
of
\mathcal{A}
on an infinite word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
.
We define the
stack height
of the
i
-th configuration as
sh(\rho(i))=|\gamma_{i}|-1
(i.e., the bottom symbol
\bot
does not count to the stack height).
The stair-parity condition relies on the notion of
steps
:

Definition 2.3

(Step)
.

Let
\rho
be a run of
\mathcal{A}
.
Position
i\geq 0
is a
step
of
\rho
if

\forall n\geq i\colon\quad sh(\rho(n))~\geq~sh(\rho(i))~.

Intuitively, a step is a position of a run such that there is no future position where the stack height is strictly smaller.
Slightly abusing terminology, we also say that a
configuration

s_{i}\gamma_{i}
of a given run
\rho=s_{0}\gamma_{0},s_{1}\gamma_{1},\ldots
is a step if position
i
is a step.

Example 2.4
.

Figure

4
depicts a DVPA and the initial fragment of its unique run
\rho
on the input word
\tau\,r\,c\,\tau\,\tau\,c\,r\,c^{2}\,r^{2}\,c^{3}\,r^{3}\ldots
.
The step positions are underlined, i.e., positions 0-5, 7, 11, and 17 are steps.
Note that if
\rho(i)=s\bot
for some
s\in S
then
i
is a step, i.e., bottom configurations are always steps.

Figure 4.

Left: An example VPA (in fact, a DVPA) with
\Gamma=\{Z,\bot\}
over input alphabet
\Sigma=\{c\}\uplus\{\tau\}\uplus\{r\}
.
Transitions labeled
c,Z
are
call transitions
that push
Z
on the stack.
The transitions labeled
\tau
are
internal transitions
; they ignore the stack completely.
Transitions labeled
Z,r
and
\bot,r
are
return transitions
that are only enabled if
Z
(
\bot
, respectively) is on top of the stack.
When executing
Z,r
, the symbol
Z
is popped from the stack.
However, the special bottom-of-stack symbol
\bot
can never be popped (e.g., position 1 in the run).
Right: The
unique
run of the DVPA on input word
\tau\,r\,c\,\tau\,\tau\,c\,r\,c^{2}\,r^{2}\,c^{3}\,r^{3}\ldots
.
Steps are underlined.

Steps play a central role in the rest of the paper.
We therefore explain some of their fundamental properties.
Suppose that
\rho
is a run of
\mathcal{A}
on an infinite word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.

•

If positions
i<j
are
adjacent
steps, i.e., there is no step
k
strictly in between
i
and
j
, then
sh(\rho(j))-sh(\rho(i))\in\{0,1\}
, i.e., the stack height from one step to the next increases by either zero or one.

•

Each step
i
has a
next
step
j>i
:
If the symbol at step
i
is internal (e.g.,
i=0,3,4
in
Figure

4
) or a return (e.g.,
i=1
) then the next step is simply the next position
j=i+1
and the stack height does not increase.
If the symbol at position
i
is a call, then one of two cases occurs:
Either the call has no matching future return (e.g.,
i=2
);
in this case, the next step is the next position
j=i+1
.
Otherwise the call is eventually matched (e.g.,
i=5,7,11
) and the next step
j>i+1
occurs after the corresponding matching return is read and has the same stack height.

•

As a consequence, each infinite run has infinitely many steps.
Notice though that the difference between two adjacent step positions may grow unboundedly as in
Figure

4
.

•

The stack height at the steps either grows unboundedly or eventually stabilizes (the latter occurs in
Figure

4
; the stack heights at the steps induce the sequence
0,0,0,1^{\omega}
).

Remark 2.5
.

We can also define the steps of a
word

w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
as the positions where a run of
any arbitrary
VPA on
w
has a step.
Due to the visibility restriction, the actual behavior of the VPA does not influence the step positions
[
vpGames
]
.
In other words, the step positions are predetermined by the input word.
Thus, we can also speak of the stack height
sh(w(i))
of word
w
at position
i
.

We need one last notion before defining stair-parity.
The
footprint
of an infinite run
\rho=s_{0}\gamma_{0},s_{1}\gamma_{1},\ldots
is the infinite sequence
\rho{\downarrow}_{\mathit{Steps}}=s_{n_{0}}s_{n_{1}}\ldots\in S^{\omega}
where for all
i\geq 0
the position
n_{i}
is the
i
-th step of
\rho
.
In words,
\rho{\downarrow}_{\mathit{Steps}}
is the projection of the run
\rho
onto the
states
occurring at its steps.
For the example run in
Figure

4
,
\rho{\downarrow}_{\mathit{Steps}}=s_{0}s_{1}s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}^{\omega}
.

Definition 2.6

(Stair-parity
[
vpGames
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a VPA over pushdown alphabet
\Sigma
.
A
stair-parity
acceptance condition for
\mathcal{A}
is defined in terms of a priority function
\Omega\colon S\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
A word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
is accepted if
\mathcal{A}
has a run
\rho
on
w
such that

\min\,\{\,k\,\mid\,\overset{\infty}{\exists}i\geq 0\colon~\Omega(\,\rho{\downarrow}_{\mathit{Steps}}(i)\,)\,=\,k\,\}\quad\text{is even.}

The language accepted by
\mathcal{A}
is denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

\triangle

Intuitively,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
contains all words that have a run
\rho
on
\mathcal{A}
such that the minimum priority occurring infinitely often at states in the
footprint

\rho{\downarrow}_{\mathit{Steps}}
is even.

Example 2.7
.

The DVPA in
Figure

4
with
\Omega(s_{0})=1
and
\Omega(s_{1})=2
accepts

\mathcal{L}_{\mathit{repbdd}}~=~\{\,w\in\Sigma^{\omega}\,\mid\,\exists B\geq 0,\,\overset{\infty}{\exists}i\geq 0\colon~sh(w(i))\,=\,B\,\}~,

the language of
repeatedly bounded
words
[
vpGames
]
, i.e., words whose stack height (cf.
Remark

2.5
) is infinitely often equal to some constant
B
.
The example word from
Figure

4
satisfies this property with
B=1
.
To see why the automaton accepts
\mathcal{L}_{\mathit{repbdd}}
, note that a word is repeatedly bounded iff the stack height at the steps stabilizes eventually.
The latter occurs iff in just finitely many cases, the transition before reaching a step was a call.
The DVPA in
Figure

4
detects this behavior; when reading a call symbol, it always moves to state
s_{0}
which has odd priority, and it accepts iff
s_{0}
is visited finitely often
at call positions
.
It is known that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathit{repbdd}}
is not expressible through DVPA with usual parity conditions
[
vpl
]
.

{thmC}

[
[
vpGames
, Theorem 1]
]

For every non-deterministic Büchi VPA
\mathcal{A}
there exists a deterministic stair-parity DVPA
\mathcal{D}
with
2^{\mathcal{O}(|S|^{2})}
states such that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D})
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{D}
can be constructed in exponential time in the size of
\mathcal{A}
.

It was also shown in
[
vpGames
]
that stair-parity DVPA characterize exactly the class of
\omega
-VPL (and are thus not more expressive than non-deterministic Büchi VPA).

2.3.
CaRet, a Temporal Logic of Calls and Returns

Specifying requirements directly in terms of automata is tedious in practice.

CaRet

[
caret
]
is an extension of Linear Temporal Logic (LTL)
[
DBLP:conf/focs/Pnueli77
]
that can be used to describe certain
\omega
-VPL.

Definition 2.8

(Syntax of CaRet
[
caret
]
)
.

Let
{AP}
be a finite set of atomic propositions.
The logic CaRet adheres to the grammar

\varphi~\coloneqq~p\,\mid\,\varphi\lor\varphi\,\mid\,\neg\varphi\,\mid\,\bigcirc^{g}\varphi\,\mid\,\varphi\,\mathcal{U}^{g}\varphi\,\mid\,\bigcirc^{a}\varphi\,\mid\,\varphi\,\mathcal{U}^{a}\varphi\,\mid\,\bigcirc^{-}\varphi\,\mid\,\varphi\,\mathcal{U}^{-}\varphi~,

where
p\in{AP}\cup\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}
.

\triangle

Other common modalities such as
\lozenge^{b}
and
\square^{b}
for
b\in\{g,a,-\}
are defined as usual via
\lozenge^{b}\varphi=\mathit{true}\,\mathcal{U}^{b}\,\varphi
, and
\square^{b}\varphi=\neg\lozenge^{b}\neg\varphi
.
We now explain the intuitive semantics of CaRet, the formal definition is stated further below in
Definition

2.9
.
We assume familiarity with LTL (see, e.g.,
[
MCbible
, Ch. 5]
for an introduction).
CaRet formulae are interpreted over infinite words from the pushdown alphabet
\Sigma=2^{{AP}}\times\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}
.

\bigcirc^{g}
and
\mathcal{U}^{g}
are the standard next and until modalities from LTL (called
global
next and until in CaRet).
CaRet extends LTL by two key operators, the
caller
modality
\bigcirc^{-}
and the
abstract successor

\bigcirc^{a}
.
The semantics of these operators is visually explained in
Figure

5
.
The caller
\bigcirc^{-}
is a
past
modality that points to the position of the last pending call (if such a call exists).
For internal and return symbols, the abstract successor
\bigcirc^{a}
behaves like
\bigcirc^{g}
unless the latter is a return, in which case
\bigcirc^{a}
is undefined (e.g., position 3 in
Figure

5
).
On the other hand, the abstract successor of a call symbol is its
matching return
if it exists, or undefined otherwise.
The caller and abstract successor modalities induce sequences of positions which we call
caller path
and
abstract path
, respectively.
The caller path is always finite and the abstract path can be either finite or infinite.
The until modalities
\mathcal{U}^{-}
and
\mathcal{U}^{a}
are then defined analogously to the standard until
\mathcal{U}^{g}
with the difference that they are interpreted over the caller and abstract path, respectively.

A prime application of CaRet is to express
total correctness
of a procedure
F

[
caret
]
:

\varphi_{\mathit{total}}\quad=\quad\square^{g}\,(\,\mathsf{call}\,\land\,p\,\land\,p_{F}~\rightarrow~\bigcirc^{a}q\,)

where
p
and
q
are atomic propositions that hold at the states where the pre- and post-condition is satisfied, respectively, and
p_{F}
is an atomic proposition marking the calls to
F
.
Another example is the language of repeatedly bounded words from
Example

2.7
; it is described by the formula
\varphi_{\mathit{repbdd}}=\lozenge^{g}\square^{g}(\mathsf{call}\rightarrow\bigcirc^{a}\mathsf{ret})
which states that all but finitely many calls have a matching return.
Further examples are given in
[
caret
]
.

0
1
2
3
4
5
...
\bigcirc^{g}
\mathsf{call}
\bigcirc^{g/a}
\mathsf{int}
\bigcirc^{-}
\bigcirc^{g}
\mathsf{call}
\bigcirc^{-}
\bigcirc^{a}
\bigcirc^{g}
\mathsf{int}
\bigcirc^{-}
\bigcirc^{g/a}
\mathsf{ret}
\bigcirc^{-}
\bigcirc^{-}

Figure 5.

CaRet’s various next modalities applied to the initial fragment of an example word.
Call, internal, and return positions are depicted as boxes, circles, and rhombs, respectively.
Note that
\bigcirc^{a}
of position 3 is undefined because
\bigcirc^{g}
is a return.
Whether or not
\bigcirc^{a}
of position 0 is defined depends on how the words continues after position 5; more specifically, it is defined iff there occurs a return position on the same height as position 5. In this case,
\bigcirc^{a}
of position 0 will point to the first such occurrence.

We now define the semantics of CaRet formally.
Let
\Sigma=2^{AP}\times\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}
be the pushdown alphabet and
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
.
If
w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}
, then let
\mathit{MR}_{w}(i)
be the position of the matching return of
w(i)
, or
\mathit{undef}
if there is no matching return.
The
abstract successor

\mathit{succ}^{a}_{w}(i)
of position
i
in word
w
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathit{succ}^{a}_{w}(i)~=~\begin{cases}\mathit{MR}_{w}(i)&\text{ if }w(i)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}\\
i+1&\text{ if }w(i)\notin\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}~\land~w(i{+}1)\notin\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}\\
\mathit{undef}&\text{ if }w(i)\notin\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}~\land~w(i{+}1)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}\\
\end{cases}

The
caller

\mathit{succ}^{-}_{w}(i)
of position
i
in word
w
is defined as the innermost (“closest”) unmatched call position
j<i
, or
\mathit{undef}
if there was no previous open call.

Definition 2.9

(Semantics of CaRet)
.

Let
\Sigma=2^{AP}\times\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}
and
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
.
For all CaRet formulae
\varphi
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define
w(i)\models\varphi
by induction over the structure of
\varphi
as follows:

•

w(i)\models p
iff
w(i)=(X,\mathsf{type})
and either
p\in X
or
p=\mathsf{type}

•

w(i)\models\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
iff
w(i)\models\varphi_{1}
or
w(i)\models\varphi_{2}

•

w(i)\models\neg\varphi
iff
w(i)\nvDash\varphi

•

w(i)\models\bigcirc^{g}\varphi_{1}
iff
w(i+1)\models\varphi_{1}

•

w(i)\models\bigcirc^{a}\varphi_{1}
iff
\mathit{succ}^{a}_{w}(i)=j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is defined and
w(j)\models\varphi_{1}

•

w(i)\models\bigcirc^{-}\varphi_{1}
iff
\mathit{succ}^{-}_{w}(i)=j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is defined and
w(j)\models\varphi_{1}

•

w(i)\models\varphi_{1}\,\mathcal{U}^{b}\varphi_{2}
for
b\in\{a,g,-\}
if there exist positions
i=i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that

(1)

w(i_{k})\models\varphi_{2}
, and

(2)

for all
0\leq j<k
,
w(i_{j})\models\varphi_{1}
and
i_{j+1}=\mathit{succ}^{b}_{w}(i_{j})
.

Furthermore, we write
w\models\varphi
if
w(0)\models\varphi
.
The language of all words satisfying a CaRet formula
\varphi
is denoted
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\{w\in\Sigma^{\omega}\mid w\models\varphi\}
.

\triangle

{thmC}

[
[
DBLP:conf/lics/AlurABEIL07
, Theorem 5.1]
]

CaRet-definable languages are
\omega
-VPL:
For each CaRet formula
\varphi
there exists a (non-deterministic) Büchi VPA
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\mathcal{A}
can be constructed in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)}
.

The above theorem is well-known in the literature
[
DBLP:conf/lics/AlurABEIL07
,
DBLP:reference/mc/AlurBE18
]
even though it is usually stated for
Nested Word Automata
(NWA) which are equivalent to VPA, and it is more common to state a space bound on
\mathcal{A}
rather than a time bound for the construction.
The theorem even applies to the logic NWTL
+
, an FO-complete extension of CaRet
[
DBLP:conf/lics/AlurABEIL07
]
which we do not consider here for the sake of simplicity.

Sections

2.3
and
2.2
together imply that each CaRet formula can be translated to a
deterministic
stair-parity VPA of doubly-exponential size.

3.
Probabilistic Visibly Pushdown Automata

As explained in the introduction, we employ
probabilistic pushdown automata

[
esparzaMCPPDA-lics
]
(pPDA) as an operational model for procedural probabilistic programs.
pPDA thus play a fundamentally different role in this paper than VPA (cf.
Definition

2.1
):
pPDA are used to model the
system
, while VPA encode the
specification
.
Consequently, our pPDA do
not
read an input word like VPA do, but instead take their transitions randomly, according to fixed probability distributions.
In this way, they define a probability space over their possible traces, i.e., runs projected on their labeling sequence.
These traces constitute the input words of the VPA.
In order for the model checking problems to be decidable
[
dubslaff
]
, a syntactic visibility restriction needs to be imposed on pPDA.
In a nutshell, the condition is that each state only has outgoing transitions of one
type
, i.e., push, internal, or pop.
This means that the stack operation is
visible in the states
(recall that for VPA, the stack operation is visible in the input symbol).
This restriction is not severe in the context of modeling programs (see
further below)
and leads to our notion of probabilistic
visibly
pushdown automata (pVPA) which we now define formally.

Given a finite set
X
, we write
\mathit{Dist}(X)=\{\,f\colon X\to[0,1]\,\mid\,\sum_{a\in X}f(a)=1\,\}
for the set of probability distributions over
X
.

Definition 3.1

(pVPA)
.

A
probabilistic visibly pushdown automaton
(pVPA) is a tuple
\Delta=(Q,\,q_{0},\,\Gamma,\,\bot,\,P,\,\Sigma,\,\lambda)
where

Q
is a finite set of states partitioned into
Q=Q_{\mathsf{call}}\uplus Q_{\mathsf{int}}\uplus Q_{\mathsf{ret}}
,

q_{0}\in Q
is an initial state,

\Gamma
is a finite stack alphabet,

\bot\in\Gamma
is a special bottom-of-stack symbol,

P=(P_{\mathsf{call}},P_{\mathsf{int}},P_{\mathsf{ret}})
is a triple of functions

P_{\mathsf{call}}\colon Q_{\mathsf{call}}\to\mathit{Dist}(Q\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot})~,\quad P_{\mathsf{int}}\colon Q_{\mathsf{int}}\to\mathit{Dist}(Q)~,\quad P_{\mathsf{ret}}\colon Q_{\mathsf{ret}}\times\Gamma\to\mathit{Dist}(Q)~,

\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathsf{call}}\uplus\Sigma_{\mathsf{int}}\uplus\Sigma_{\mathsf{ret}}
is a pushdown alphabet,
and
\lambda\colon Q\to\Sigma
is a state labeling function consistent with the visibility condition, i.e., for all
\mathsf{type}\in\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}
and all
q\in Q
, it is required that

q\in Q_{\mathsf{type}}
iff
\lambda(q)\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{type}}
.

\triangle

Similar to VPA, we use the notation
q\xrightarrow{\prob}rZ$,$q \xrightarrow{\prob}r$,and$qZ \xrightarrow{\prob}r$toindicatethat$P_{\mathsf{call}}(q)(r,Z) = \prob$,$P_{\mathsf{int}}(q)(r) = \prob$,and$P_{\mathsf{ret}}(q,Z)(r) = \prob$,respectively.\par Intuitively,thebehaviorofapVPA$\Delta$isasfollows.Ifthecurrentstate$q$isacallstate,thentheprobabilitydistribution$P_{\mathsf{call}}(q)$determinesarandomsuccessorstateandstacksymboltobepushedonthestack($\bot$cannotbepushed).Similarly,ifthecurrentstate$q$isinternal,then$P_{\mathsf{int}}(q)$isthedistributionoverpossiblesuccessorstatesandnostackoperationisperformed.Lastly,ifthecurrentstate$q$isareturnstateandsymbol$Z \in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}$isontopofthestack,then$Z$ispoppedand$\Delta$movestoasuccessorstatewithprobabilityaccordingto$P_{\mathsf{ret}}(q, Z)$.AsinVPA,thespecialsymbol$\bot$is\emph{not}poppedfromthestackifareturntransitionoccursinabottomconfiguration.\par TheformalsemanticsofapVPAisdefinedintermsofacountablyinfinitediscrete-time\emph{Markovchain}.A(labeled)MarkovchainisessentiallythespecialcaseofapVPAwhere$Q= Q_{\mathsf{int}}$,withtheonlydifferencethatweallowforcountablyinfinite$Q$anddonotimposetherestrictiononthelabelingfunction$\lambda$.AMarkovchaincanthusbespecifiedasa5-tuple$(Q, q_{0}, P, \Sigma, \lambda)$,i.e.,weomit$\bot$and$\Gamma$fromthedefinitionofpVPAbecauseaMarkovchaindoesnotuseastack.A\emph{run}ofaMarkovchainisaninfinitesequenceofstates,i.e.,anelementfrom$Q^{\omega}$.Notethatinourdefinition,runsdonotnecessarilystartin$q_{0}$;thisisjustfortechnicalconvenience---impossiblerunsstartinginastateotherthan$q_{0}$willsimplyhaveprobability$0$.Weextendthelabelingfunction$\lambda$fromstatestorunsinthenaturalway.\par Wedefinethe\emph{MarkovchaingeneratedbyapVPA}$\Delta= (Q, \, q_{0}, \, \Gamma, \, \bot, \, P, \, \Sigma, \, \lambda)$as$$\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}~=~(\,Q\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{*}.\bot,\,q_{0}\bot,\,P_{\Delta},\,\Sigma,\,\lambda_{\Delta}\,)~,$$i.e.,thestatespaceof$\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$isthesetofconfigurationsof$\Delta$,andthetransitionprobabilityfunction$P_{\Delta}$isdefinedasfollows.$P_{\Delta}(q\gamma)(r\gamma') = p > 0$iffexactlyoneofthefollowingcasesapplies:\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$q\in Q_{\mathsf{call}}$ and $\gamma^{\prime}=Z\gamma$ for some $Z\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}$ and $q\xrightarrow{p}rZ$; or
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$ and $\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma$ and $q\xrightarrow{p}r$; or
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$q\in Q_{\mathsf{ret}}$ and $Z\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma$ for some $Z\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}$ and $qZ\xrightarrow{p}q$; or
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$q\in Q_{\mathsf{ret}}$ and
$\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma=\bot$ and $q\bot\xrightarrow{p}r$.
\end{itemize}Moreover,thelabelingfunctionof$\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$is$\lambda_{\Delta}(q\gamma) = \lambda(q)$forall$q \gamma\in Q\times(\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot})^{*}.\bot$.\par\begin{example}\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:inducedMCexample} depicts a pVPA $\Delta$ and a fragment of its generated Markov chain $\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$.
Even though $\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$ is infinite, many problems remain decidable, including in particular questions about reachability probabilities which can be characterized as the least solution of a system of polynomial equations~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}.
We will use this extensively in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:modelCheckingDVPA}.

Left: A pVPA $\Delta$ (labeling function omitted) with $\Gamma=\{\bot,Z\}$.
Call and return states are drawn as squares and rhombs, respectively.
Transitions labeled $p,Z$ for $p\in[0,1]$ mean that with probability $p$ symbol $Z$ is pushed.
Transitions labeled $X,p$, for $X\in\Gamma$ and $p\in[0,1]$, indicate that \emph{if} $X$ is on top of the stack, then the transition is taken with probability $p$ (and $X$ is popped if $X\neq\bot$).
Note that the visibility restriction on pVPA enforces that a state may not have both outgoing push and outgoing pop transitions.
Right: The infinite-state Markov chain $\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$ generated by $\Delta$.
}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 6}}{
Left: A pVPA $\Delta$ (labeling function omitted) with $\Gamma=\{\bot,Z\}$.
Call and return states are drawn as squares and rhombs, respectively.
Transitions labeled $p,Z$ for $p\in[0,1]$ mean that with probability $p$ symbol $Z$ is pushed.
Transitions labeled $X,p$, for $X\in\Gamma$ and $p\in[0,1]$, indicate that \emph{if} $X$ is on top of the stack, then the transition is taken with probability $p$ (and $X$ is popped if $X\neq\bot$).
Note that the visibility restriction on pVPA enforces that a state may not have both outgoing push and outgoing pop transitions.
Right: The infinite-state Markov chain $\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$ generated by $\Delta$.

\@add@centering\end{figure}\par Wedefinetheset$Runs_{\Delta}$ofapVPA$\Delta$astherunsoftheMarkovchain$\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$,i.e.,$Runs_{\Delta} = (Q\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{*}.\bot)^{\omega}$.\emph{Steps}ofpVPArunsaredefinedasin\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:step}.AfurtherexamplepVPAanditspossiblerunsaredepictedin\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:step-mc-simple}onpage~\ref{fig:step-mc-simple}.\par Weassociateaprobabilityspacewith$Runs_{\Delta}$intheusualway(see,e.g.,~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{MCbible}{}{}, Ch.~10]}).Tothisend,wedefinethe$\sigma$-algebra$\mathcal{F} \subseteq 2^{Runs_{\Delta}}$asthesmallestsetthatcontainsallthe\emph{cylindersets}$\rho.Runs_{\Delta}$,where$\rho$isanarbitrary\emph{finite}prefix$\rho\in(Q\times\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{*}.\bot)^{*}$ofarun,andthatisclosedundercomplementandcountableunion.Thesetsin$\mathcal{F}$arecalled\emph{measurable}andthereisauniqueprobabilitymeasure$\mathbb{P}_{\Delta} \colon\mathcal{F} \to[0,1]$satisfying$$\mathbb{P}_{\Delta}(\rho.Runs_{\Delta})~=~\begin{cases}\prod_{i=0}^{|\rho|-2}P_{\Delta}(\rho(i),\rho(i{+}1))&\text{ if }|\rho|=0\text{ or }(|\rho|>0\text{ and }\rho(0)=q_{0}\bot),\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}$$whereanemptyproduct(whichoccursif$|\rho| \leq 1$)isdefinedtobeequalto$1$.Weomitthesubscriptin$\mathbb{P}_{\Delta}$whenever$\Delta$isgivenbythecontext.\par Inthefollowingtworemarks,wesummarizethetechnicaldifferencesbetweenourpVPAmodelandexistingmodelsintheliterature.\par\begin{remark}Unlike the pPDA from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}, our pVPA only generate \emph{infinite} runs, i.e., they do not ``terminate'' when reaching the empty stack.
Indeed, in our pVPA, the stack can never be empty because the special bottom symbol $\bot$ cannot be popped.
We have chosen this semantics for compatibility with CaRet which describes $\omega$-languages by definition.
Nonetheless, terminating behavior can be easily simulated in our framework by moving to a dedicated sink state once the pVPA attempts to pop $\bot$ for the first time.
Another technical difference between our pVPA and the pPDA introduced in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]} is that in pVPA, only pop transitions can read the stack, whereas in pPDA, all types of transitions can read, and possibly exchange, the current topmost stack symbol.
We have chosen this definition (which is not a true restriction) for compatibility with VPA as defined in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{vpl}{}{}]}.
\end{remark}\par\begin{remark}The visibility restriction of our pVPA is slightly different from the definition given in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{dubslaff}{}{}]} which requires all \emph{incoming} transitions to a state to be of the same type, i.e., call, internal, or return.
Our definition, on the other hand, imposes the same requirement on the states' \emph{outgoing} transitions.
We believe that our condition is more natural for pVPA obtained from procedural programs, such as the one in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:exampleProg}.
In fact, programs where randomness is restricted to \emph{internal} statements such as $x:=\texttt{bernoulli}(0.5)$ or $x:=\texttt{uniform}(0,3)$ naturally comply with our visibility condition because all call and return states of such programs are deterministic and thus cannot violate visibility.
However, the alternative condition of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{dubslaff}{}{}]} is not necessarily fulfilled for such programs.
\end{remark}\par Wecannowformallystateourmainproblemofinterest:\begin{definition}Let ${AP}$ be a finite set of atomic propositions,
$\varphi$ be a CaRet formula over ${AP}$,
$\Delta$ be a pVPA with labels from the pushdown alphabet $\Sigma=2^{AP}\times\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}$,
and $\theta\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}$.
The \emph{quantitative CaRet Model Checking problem} is to decide whether
$$\mathbb{P}(\{\,\rho\in Runs_{\Delta}\,\mid\,\lambda(\rho)\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\,\})~\geq_{?}~\theta~.$$The \emph{qualitative} CaRet Model Checking problem is the special case where $\theta=1$.
\hfill$\triangle$\end{definition}Theprobabilitiesin\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:caretDecisionProblems}arewell-definedas$\omega$-VPLaremeasurable~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{vpGames}{}{}]}.\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Model Checking pVPA against Stair-parity DVPA}
\par In this section, we show that model checking pVPA (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:pVPA}) against VPL given in terms of a stair-parity DVPA (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:stairParity}) is decidable.
This is achieved by first computing an automata-theoretic product of the pVPA and the DVPA and then evaluating the acceptance condition in the product automaton.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Products of Visibly Pushdown Automata}
\par In general, pushdown automata are not closed under taking products as this would require \emph{two} independent stacks.
However, the visibility conditions on VPA and pVPA ensure that their product is again an automaton with just a single stack because the stack operations (push, internal, or pop) are forced to synchronize.
\par We now define the product formally.
An \emph{unlabeled} pVPA is a pVPA where the labeling function $\lambda$ and alphabet $\Sigma$ are omitted.
\par\par\begin{definition}Let $\Delta=(Q,\,q_{0},\,\Gamma,\,\bot,\,P,\,\Sigma,\,\lambda)$ be a pVPA, and $\mathcal{D}=(S,\,s_{0},\,\Gamma^{\prime},\,\bot,\,\delta,\,\Sigma)$ be a DVPA over pushdown alphabet $\Sigma$.
The product of $\Delta$ and $\mathcal{D}$ is the unlabeled pVPA
\@@amsalign
where $P_{\Delta\times\mathcal{D}}$ is the smallest set of transitions satisfying the following rules for all $q,r\in Q$, $Z\in\Gamma$, $s,t\in S$, and $Y\in\Gamma^{\prime}$:
\@@amsgather
If the DVPA $\mathcal{D}$ is equipped with a priority function $\Omega\colon S\to\mathbb{N}_{0}$, then we extend $\Omega$ to $\Omega^{\prime}\colon Q\times S\to\mathbb{N}_{0}$ via $\Omega^{\prime}(q,s)=\Omega(s)$.
\hfill$\triangle$\end{definition}It is not difficult to show that $\Delta\times\mathcal{D}$ is indeed a well-defined pVPA and moreover satisfies the following property (the proof is standard, see~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{arxivV2}{}{}, Appendix B.1]}):
\par\restatable[Soundness of $\Delta\times\mathcal{D}$]{lemma}{restateProdCorrect}
Let $\Delta$ be a pVPA and $\mathcal{D}$ be a stair-parity DVPA with priority function $\Omega$, both over pushdown alphabet $\Sigma$.
Then the product pVPA $\Delta\times\mathcal{D}$ with priority function $\Omega^{\prime}$ as in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:prod} satisfies
\@@amsalign
where $\mathit{Parity}_{\Omega^{\prime}}$ denotes the set of words in $(Q\times S)^{\omega}$ satisfying the \emph{standard} parity condition induced by $\Omega^{\prime}$.
Moreover, $\Delta\times\mathcal{D}$ can be constructed in polynomial time.
\par\begin{remark}It is \emph{not} actually important that the product pVPA again satisfies the visibility condition, even though this happens to be the case.
All techniques we apply to the product also work for general pPDA.
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Stair-parity Acceptance Probabilities in pVPA}
\par\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:product} effectively reduces model checking pVPA against stair-parity DVPA to computing stair-parity acceptance in the product, which is again an (unlabeled) pVPA.
We therefore focus on pVPA in this section and do not consider DVPA.
\par Throughout the rest of this section, let $\Delta=(\,Q,\,q_{0},\,\Gamma,\,\bot,\,P\,)$ be an unlabeled pVPA.
On the next pages we describe the construction of a \emph{finite} Markov chain $\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}$ that we call the \emph{step chain} of $\Delta$.
Loosely speaking, $\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}$ simulates jumping from one \emph{step} (see \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:step}) of a run of $\Delta$ to the next.
\par\begin{remark}The idea of the step Markov chain $\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}$ first appeared in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}.
However, the step chain as presented here differs from the original definition in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]} in at least two important aspects.
First, we have to take the semantics of our special bottom symbol $\bot$ into account.
This is why our chain uses a subset of $Q\cup Q\bot$ as states---it must distinguish whether a step occurs at a bottom configuration.
The pPDA in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}, on the other hand, may have both finite and infinite runs, and this needs to be handled differently in the step chain.
Second, we use step chains for a different purpose than~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}, namely to show that general measurable properties defined on steps---this includes stair-parity---can be evaluated on pVPA (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:stepChainSound}).
\end{remark}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Steps as events.}
\par For all $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, we define a \emph{random} variable $V^{(n)}$ on $Runs_{\Delta}$ whose value is either the state $q$ of $\Delta$ at the $n$-th step, or the \emph{extended state} $q\bot$ in the special case where the $n$-th step occurs at a bottom configuration $q\bot$, for some $q\in Q$.
We denote the set of all such extended states with $Q\bot=\{\,q\bot\,\mid\,q\in Q\,\}$.
Formally, $V^{(n)}\colon Runs_{\Delta}\to Q\cup Q\bot$ is defined as
$$V^{(n)}(\rho)\hskip 3.5pt=\hskip 3.5pt\begin{cases}q&\text{ if }\mathit{step}_{n}(\rho)=q\gamma\text{ and }\gamma\neq\bot\\
q\bot&\text{ if }\mathit{step}_{n}(\rho)=q\bot~,\\
\end{cases}$$where $\mathit{step}_{n}(\rho)$ denotes the configuration at the $n$-th step of $\rho$.
\par\restatable{lemma}{restateVwellDef}
For all $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $v\in Q\cup Q\bot$, the event $V^{(n)}=v$ is measurable, and thus $V^{(n)}$ is a well-defined random variable.
\begin{proof}This was proved more generally in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}.
Here we give an alternative proof using the fact that all $\omega$-VPL are measurable~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{vpGames}{}{}]}.
We view $Q\cup Q\bot$ as a pushdown alphabet (the partition is induced by the partition on $Q$).
We can construct a non-deterministic Büchi VPA that accepts a word $w\in(Q\cup Q\bot)^{\omega}$ iff the $n$-th step of $w$ is $v$ (the size of this automaton depends on $n$).
To this end, the VPA guesses the first $n$ positions that are steps and goes to an accepting state $s$ if the $n$-th step was $v$.
The automaton can verify that it guessed correctly as follows.
If it believes a call symbol is a step, it pushes a special marker on the stack; if this marker is ever popped, then the call was no step and the guess was wrong.
If it detects a wrong guess in this way, then it leaves the accepting state $s$, otherwise it loops there forever.
The claim follows because the function $f\colon Runs_{\Delta}\to(Q\cup Q\bot)^{\omega}$ that maps runs to the sequence of their (extended) states is measurable (indeed, the preimage $f^{-1}(w)$ of every $w\in(Q\cup Q\bot)^{\omega}$ is
$$f^{-1}(w)~=~\bigcap_{i\geq 0}~\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{0}\ldots\gamma_{i}\,\in\,\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{*}.\bot\end{subarray}}w(0)\gamma_{0}\,\ldots\,w(i)\gamma_{i}.Runs_{\Delta}$$which is a countable intersection of countable unions of basic cylinder sets).
\end{proof}\par\par We can view the sequence $V^{(0)},V^{(1)}\ldots$ of random variables as a \emph{stochastic process}.
It is intuitively clear that for all $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, the value of $V^{(n+1)}$ depends only on $V^{(n)}$, but not on $V^{(i)}$ for $i<n$.
This is due to the more general observation that only the \emph{state} $q$ at any step configuration $q\gamma$ (with $\gamma\neq\bot$) \emph{fully determines the future of the run} because being a step already implies that no symbol in $\gamma$ can ever be read, as reading it implies popping it from the stack.
In particular, $q$ determines the probability distribution over possible next steps.
A similar observation applies to bottom configurations of the form $q\bot$.
Phrased in the language of probability theory, the process $V^{(0)},V^{(1)}\ldots$ has the \emph{Markov property}, i.e.,
\begin{equation}\mathbb{P}(V^{(n)}=v_{n}\mid V^{(n-1)}=v_{n-1}\,\wedge\,\ldots\,\wedge\,V^{(0)}=v_{0})~=~\mathbb{P}(V^{(n)}=v_{n}\mid V^{(n-1)}=v_{n-1})\end{equation}holds for all values of $v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}$ such that the conditional probabilities are well-defined.
This was proved in detail in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}.
It is also clear that the Markov process is time-homogeneous in the sense that
\@@amsalign
holds for all $n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ for which the two conditional probabilities are well-defined.
The following example provides some intuition on these facts.
\begin{example}Consider the pVPA in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:step-mc-simple} (left).
The initial fragments of its two equiprobable runs are depicted in the middle.
In this example, it is easy to read off the next-step probabilities $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n)}=v_{n}\mid V^{(n-1)}=v_{n-1})$ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $v_{n},v_{n-1}\in Q\cup Q\bot$.
They are summarized in the Markov chain on the right.
For example, $V^{(0)}=q_{0}\bot$ holds with probability 1, and $V^{(1)}=q_{1}$ and $V^{(1)}=q_{3}\bot$ hold with probability $\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}$ each because the second step occurs either at position $1$ with configuration $q_{1}\bot Z$ or at position $3$ with configuration $q_{3}\bot$, and both options are equally likely.
The case $\mathbb{P}(V^{(2)}=q_{2}\mid V^{(1)}=q_{1})=1$ is slightly more interesting:
Given that a configuration $q_{1}\gamma$ with $\gamma\neq\bot$ is a step, we know that the next state must be $q_{2}$ (which is then also a step).
Even though there is a transition from $q_{1}$ to $q_{3}$ in $\Delta$, the next state cannot be $q_{3}$ because the latter is a return state which would immediately decrease the stack height of $\gamma$.
This shows that, intuitively speaking, \emph{conditioning on being a step influences the probabilities of a state's outgoing transitions}.
\end{example}\par\begin{figure}\centering\begin{minipage}[151.80084pt]\hbox to148.1pt{\vbox to70.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.5811pt\lower-35.6831pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Probabilities of next steps, returns, and diverges.}
\par Our next goal is to provide expressions for the next-step probabilities $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=v^{\prime}\mid V^{(n)}=v)$ as we did in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:stepChainSimple}.
It turns out that those can be stated in terms of the \emph{return} and \emph{diverge} probabilities of $\Delta$.
\par\begin{definition}Let $q,r\in Q$ be states, and $Z\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}$ be a stack symbol of pVPA $\Delta$.
We define the following probabilities:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The \emph{return} probability $[qZ{\downarrow}r]$ is the probability to reach configuration $r\bot$ from $qZ\bot$ without visiting another bottom configuration $t\bot$ for some $t\neq r$ in between.
Formally,
$$[qZ{\downarrow}r]~=~\mathbb{P}_{qZ\bot}(\,\{\,q^{\prime}\gamma\bot\,\mid\,q^{\prime}\in Q,\gamma\in\Gamma_{\text{-}\bot}^{+}\,\}~\mathcal{U}~\{r\bot\}\,)$$where $\mathbb{P}_{qZ\bot}$ is the probability measure associated with the infinite Markov chain $\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}$ assuming initial state $qZ\bot$, and $\mathcal{U}$ is the standard until operator from LTL.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The \emph{diverge} probability $[q{\uparrow}]=1-\sum_{t\in Q}[qZ{\downarrow}t]$, i.e., the probability to never pop $Z$ from the stack when starting in $qZ\bot$.
Note that $[q{\uparrow}]$ is indeed independent of $Z$ because the only way to read $Z$ is by popping it from the stack. Recall that this is due to our specific definition of pVPA (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:pVPA}) in which only pop transitions can read from the stack just like in VPA (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:vpa}); we remark that in traditional (p)PDA, all types of transition can read ---and possibly replace--- the topmost stack symbol~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]}.
\hfill$\triangle$\end{itemize}\end{definition}\par The diverge probabilities are closely related to steps.
In fact, the probability that a non-bottom configuration with head $qZ$ is a step is equal to $[q{\uparrow}]$.
For example, in the pVPA in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:step-mc-simple} the configuration $q_{1}Z\bot$ is a step with probability $[q_{1}{\uparrow}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}$.
\par\begin{example}It is well known that the return and diverge probabilities are not necessarily rational.
We give a minimal example to illustrate this fact.
Consider the following pVPA:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.7065pt}{-0.4132pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\end{center}Intuitively, this pVPA either pops the topmost symbol with probability $\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}$, or it pushes two times the symbol $Z$.
Note that all return probabilities of the form $[\ldots{\downarrow}q_{i}]$ for $i\neq 0$ are equal to zero.
In~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{esparzaMCPPDA-lics}{}{}]} it was shown that the remaining return probabilities are the \emph{component-wise least non-negative} solution of the \emph{polynomial system}:
\@@amsalign
It follows that $[q_{0}Z{\downarrow}q_{0}]$ must be the least non-negative solution of
\@@amsalign
which is $[q_{0}Z{\downarrow}q_{0}]=\tfrac{\sqrt{5}-1}{2}\approx 0.618$, the reciprocal of the golden ratio.
\hfill$\triangle$\end{example}\par The probabilities in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:nonRationalProbs} can still be expressed by radicals (square roots, cubic roots, and so on) which allows for certain effective computations.
However, in general, the probabilities cannot even be expressed in this way.
For example, consider a pVPA that repeats the following steps until emptying its stack or getting stuck in a sink state: (i) It pushes four symbols with probability $\tfrac{1}{6}$, or (ii) pops one symbol with probability $\tfrac{1}{2}$, or (iii) gets stuck otherwise.
The resulting return probability is the least $x\geq 0$ with $x=\tfrac{1}{6}x^{5}+\tfrac{1}{2}$, which is an algebraic number not solvable by radicals~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:journals/jacm/EtessamiY09}{}{}, Theorem 3.2(1)]}.
\par\par\begin{remark}The probabilities $[qZ{\downarrow}r]$ from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:returnDiverge} were called \emph{termination probabilities} in previous work~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{brazdilSurvey}{}{}]}.
However, we believe that \emph{return} probability is more appropriate.
When modeling procedural probabilistic programs as pVPA, $[qZ{\downarrow}r]$ is just the probability to eventually \emph{return} from local state $q$ of the current procedure to local state $r$ of the calling procedure (the return address is stored on the stack in $Z$).
We believe that the term \emph{termination} probability is more adequate for referring to the quantity $\sum_{r\in Q}[q_{0}Z_{0}{\downarrow}r]$, where $Z_{0}$ is some initial stack symbol, i.e., the probability that some initial procedure indentified by $Z_{0}$ returns at all when started in local state $q_{0}$.
\end{remark}\par We now state the technical key lemma of this section, the characterization of the next step probabilities $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=v^{\prime}\mid V^{(n)}=v)$ as given in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:transProbs}.
The upcoming section is devoted to proving that the entries in the table are correct.
\par\restatable{lemma}{restateTransProbs}
The conditional next-step probabilities in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:transProbs} are correct in the sense that if $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=v^{\prime}\mid V^{(n)}=v)$ is defined for $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $v,v^{\prime}\in Q\cup Q\bot$, then it is equal to the probability in the respective column $v\to v^{\prime}$.
\par\begin{table}\centering\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{
Next-step probabilities of the step Markov chain.
$P_{\mathsf{type}}$ for $\mathsf{type}\in\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}$ are the probabilities of the pVPA's call, internal, and return transitions, respectively.
The values $[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]$ and $[q{\uparrow}]$ are the return and diverge probabilities from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:returnDiverge}.
}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Table 2}}{
Next-step probabilities of the step Markov chain.
$P_{\mathsf{type}}$ for $\mathsf{type}\in\{\mathsf{call},\mathsf{int},\mathsf{ret}\}$ are the probabilities of the pVPA's call, internal, and return transitions, respectively.
The values $[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]$ and $[q{\uparrow}]$ are the return and diverge probabilities from \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:returnDiverge}.
}}}
{\par\noindent\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\begin{tabular}[]{l c c c c }\hline\cr\hline\cr&$q\to r$&$q\bot\to r$&$q\bot\to r\bot$&$q\to r\bot$\\
\hline\cr$q\in Q_{\mathsf{call}}~$&$\displaystyle\frac{[r{\uparrow}]}{[q{\uparrow}]}\bigg(\sum_{r^{\prime},Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,r^{\prime}Z)[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]+\sum_{Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,rZ)\bigg)$&$\displaystyle\sum_{Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,rZ)[r{\uparrow}]\quad$&$\displaystyle\sum_{r^{\prime},Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,r^{\prime}Z)[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]$&0\\
\hline\cr$q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$&$\displaystyle\frac{[r{\uparrow}]}{[q{\uparrow}]}P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)$&$0$&$P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)$&0\\

\@add@centering\end{table}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Proof of \Cref{thm:transProbs}}
\par We first explain the trivial entries in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:transProbs}.
Further below, we give a self-contained proof of the two non-trivial expressions in the left-most column of \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:transProbs}.
Throughout the whole proof we fix an (unlabed) pVPA $\Delta=(Q,\,q_{0},\,\Gamma,\,\bot,\,P)$, with $P=(P_{\mathsf{call}},P_{\mathsf{int}},P_{\mathsf{ret}})$ the call, internal, and return transition functions, respectively.
The following items correspond to the trivial entries in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:transProbs} and are ordered column-by-column, from left to right:
\par\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The probability $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\mid V^{(n)}=q)$ with $q\in Q_{\mathsf{ret}}$ is never well-defined because it is impossible that steps occur at non-bottom configurations with a return state.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The probability $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\mid V^{(n)}=q\bot)$ with $q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$ is trivially zero because if $q$ is internal then the next step after a bottom configuration $q\bot$ is necessarily also a bottom configuration.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The probability $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\mid V^{(n)}=q\bot)$ with $q\in Q_{\mathsf{ret}}$ is trivially zero because if $q$ is a return state at a bottom configuration then the next step occurs at the immediate successor configuration which is a bottom configuration as well.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The probability $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\bot\mid V^{(n)}=q\bot)$ with $q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$ is straightforward because $q\bot$ and $r\bot$ are both steps and the probability that the next state after $q\bot$ is $r\bot$ is $P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The probability $\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\bot\mid V^{(n)}=q\bot)$ with $q\in Q_{\mathsf{ret}}$ is simply $P_{\mathsf{ret}}(q\bot,r)$ for the same reason as in the previous case (recall that a return state at a bottom-configuration behaves exactly like an internal one).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@All the remaining probabilities in the rightmost column ``\,$q\to r\bot$\,'' are trivially zero or ill-defined because if a step occurs at non-bottom configuration, then the next step can never occur at a bottom configuration.
\end{itemize}\par We now focus on the following non-trivial cases.
Let $r\in Q$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ be arbitrary.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@If $q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$ then,
$$\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\mid V^{(n)}=q)~=~\frac{[r{\uparrow}]}{[q{\uparrow}]}P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)~.$$\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $q\in Q_{\mathsf{call}}$ then,
$$\mathbb{P}(V^{(n+1)}=r\mid V^{(n)}=q)~=~\frac{[r{\uparrow}]}{[q{\uparrow}]}\left(\sum_{r^{\prime},Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,r^{\prime}Z)[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]+\sum_{Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,rZ)\right)~.$$\end{enumerate}The other two non-trivial cases are easier variants of case (\ref{it:eq2}), hence we omit them here (see~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{arxivV2}{}{}, p. 30]} for details).
Next we provide some intuition about cases (\ref{it:eq1}) and (\ref{it:eq2}):
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For (\ref{it:eq1}), suppose that the $n$-th step is at position $i$ of the run.
Since the $n$-th step occurs at an \emph{internal} state $q\in Q_{\mathsf{int}}$, the $n{+}1$-st step must necessarily occur immediately at position $i{+}1$.
The factor $P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)[r{\uparrow}]$ is proportional to the probability to take an (internal) transition from $q$ to $r$ and then diverge in $r$, which is necessary in order for the next configuration to be a step.
However, the values $\{\,P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)[r{\uparrow}]\,\mid\,r\in Q\,\}$ do not form a probability distribution in general.
Therefore we divide by the normalizing constant $[q{\uparrow}]=\sum_{r\in Q}P_{\mathsf{int}}(q,r)[r{\uparrow}]$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@In (\ref{it:eq2}), the two summands correspond to the following case distinction:
Since the $n$-th step occurs at the \emph{call} state $q\in Q_{\mathsf{call}}$, the $n{+}1$-st step either (i) occurs at the same stack height as the current step $n$ (which means that the current call has a matching return), or (ii) the stack height at the next step $n{+}1$ is incremented by 1 compared to the stack height at step $n$.
In case (ii), the next step occurs immediately at the next position, which is why the second summand is just the 1-step probability to go from $q$ to $r$.
In case (i), the symbols pushed by the outgoing transitions of $q$ must be eventually popped.
For instance, if we assume that $q$ takes a transition to an immediate successor $r^{\prime}$ and pushes $Z$ on the stack, then the probability that the next step occurs at $r$ is precisely the \emph{return probability} $[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]$ (see~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:returnDiverge}).
The factor $\tfrac{[r{\uparrow}]}{[q{\uparrow}]}$ is needed for similar reasons as in (\ref{it:eq1}).
\end{itemize}\par We now give the formal proofs for \eqref{it:eq1} and \eqref{it:eq2}.
In the following, we often use equations involving conditional probabilities such as $\mathbb{P}(A\mid B)=\mathbb{P}(C\mid D)$.
These conditional probabilities are not necessarily well-defined in all cases.
Therefore, the meaning of our equations is that they hold only if all probabilities involved are well-defined.
We need some definitions and (simple) lemmas first.
\par\begin{definition}Let $i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $q\in Q$.
We introduce the following events:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$q@i$ is the set of all runs $\rho\in Runs_{\Delta}$ such that $\rho(i)=q\gamma$ with $\gamma\neq\bot$, i.e., the runs whose $i$-th configuration has state $q$ and stack unequal to $\bot$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Similarly, $q\bot@i$ denotes the set of all runs $\rho\in Runs_{\Delta}$ such that $\rho(i)=q\bot$, i.e., the runs whose $i$-th configuration is a \emph{bottom} configuration with state $q$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$step@i$ denotes the set of all runs such that the $i$-th configuration is a step.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@We define $sh@i=sh(\rho(i))\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, i.e., the stack height of the $i$-th configuration. Strictly speaking, $sh@i$ is a random variable, not an event.
Note that $step@i$ is by definition equivalent to $\forall j>i\colon sh@j\geq sh@i$.
\end{itemize}These events are all measurable.
\hfill$\triangle$\end{definition}\par\begin{lemma}For all $i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, and $q\in Q$, the following identities hold:

and
\@@amsalign
\end{lemma}\begin{proof}For \eqref{eq:returnProbsStackHeights}, note that $\sum_{r^{\prime},Z}P_{\mathsf{call}}(q,r^{\prime}Z)[r^{\prime}Z{\downarrow}r]$ is the probability to go from $q$ (with empty stack) to a successor state $r^{\prime}$, push $Z\in\Gamma$ and then later reach $r$ with empty stack within finitely many steps.
If we assume that $q@i$, then this is the same as summing over all positions $k>i+1$ (we can exclude $k=i+1$ because is not possible because to push and pop $Z$ within one transition) such that $r@k$ and for all $i<j<k$ the stack height is greater than at position $i$ and $k$.
Positions $i$ and $k$ have the same stack height because in the transition from $i$ the symbol $Z$ is pushed, and $k$ is the position directly after $Z$ is popped.
In between those two transitions, the stack below $Z$ cannot change, so the stack is the same at both positions.
\par For \eqref{eq:stepStackHeights} we argue as follows:
\@@amsalign
The first equality holds because if no position between $i$ and $k$ is a step, then the stack at those positions must be higher than at position $k$.
Furthermore, since $i$ is a step, we have $sh@i\leq sh@k$; and moreover, since $i{+}1$ is not a step and $q$ is a call state, we even have $sh@i=sh@k$.
The second equality holds because if $i$ and $k$ have the same stack height and all positions between them have a higher stack, then $i$ is a step if and only of $k$ is a step.
\par Equation \eqref{eq:stepIndep} is somewhat counter-intuitive because conditioning on $step@k$ is like ``conditioning on the future'':
The stack height \emph{after} position $k$ should never be smaller than at position $k$.
Knowing that $step@k$ gives information about the (extended) state at position $k$.
However, in \eqref{eq:stepIndep} we also condition on the fact that $r@k$, i.e., at position $k$, the run is in step $r$ and the topmost stack symbol is not $\bot$.
Hence, in the context of \eqref{eq:stepIndep}, we can drop $step@k$ from the condition.
\end{proof}\par\par We conclude the proof of case \eqref{it:eq2} and thus of the whole \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:transProbs} by deriving the desired equation for the first summand in \eqref{eq:twosummands}:
{\@@amsalign}This concludes the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:transProbs}.
\qed\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{The step chain.}
